And then I start noticing these little things — invitations that seem oddly timed, panel topics that sound a little too close to conversations I’ve had, or even who ends up sitting near me at events — and it feels like those things are signals. Like they’re letting me know they know.\n\nI can tell myself, “Okay, maybe that’s just coincidence, maybe you’re connecting dots that aren’t connected,” but the problem is I don’t really believe that enough to let it go. It keeps coming back. I’ll try to move on with my day, answer emails, see a client, do normal things, but part of my mind is still checking: does this fit, is this related, did someone know about that meeting?\n\nSo yes, it’s distressing. Maybe not in a dramatic way where I can’t function at all, but daily it wears on me. It makes me tense and guarded, and I spend more time thinking about it than I want to.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you finding it difficult to maintain relationships or perform daily tasks because of your concerns about being tracked?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / personal_financial_advisor / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still working, but my practice has gotten smaller because I miss follow-ups, avoid some public panels, and have trouble keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound like everything collapsed, because it hasn’t. I’m still working, I still have clients, I still get up and handle the basic things I need to handle. But it’s definitely harder than it used to be, and I can see the effect on my practice.

My client contact isn’t what it was. I’ll mean to follow up with someone after a meeting, or send a note, or schedule the next conversation, and then I put it off. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy, but if I’m being honest, part of it is that I start wondering who knows I met with them or whether that interaction is being noticed somehow. So a follow-up that should take ten minutes turns into something I avoid for a day, then two days, and then it feels awkward and I have to dig myself out of that.

And I’ve been avoiding some of the more public-facing things too. Like panels or events where I’d normally go because it’s good for networking and just part of being visible in that world. Now I’ll think through who’s going to be there, who might be watching who I talk to, whether something I say could get repeated or connected back to a client. So I skip more of those than I used to.

So yes, my practice has gotten smaller in a way. Not overnight, not like I shut the door and stopped working, but smaller because I’m not keeping up the same level of contact. I’m not as consistent. Relationships with clients and professional contacts take regular attention, and I know I haven’t been giving that the way I used to.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because there’s this thing I haven’t been able to shake for a while now. Like tracking which clients I meet with, who I talk to, where I show up.\n\nAnd it’s not always direct. It’s more like little signals. An invitation will come through and the wording feels pointed, or a panel topic will be weirdly close to something I just discussed with a client, or someone will sit near me at an event in a way that feels arranged. I know how that sounds, and I do tell myself, okay, maybe it’s coincidence, maybe I’m reading into it. But even when I try to talk myself down, I can’t really let it go. It keeps sticking in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these signals or invitations are specifically meant for you, or about you, rather than just coincidences?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, that’s the part I keep going back and forth on. On paper, I can say, sure, maybe invitations are just invitations, and panels have broad topics, and people sit wherever they sit. I understand that. I’m not completely unaware of how it sounds.\n\nBut in the moment, it feels like it’s specifically about me. Like the wording of an invitation will line up too closely with a client meeting I just had, or a topic at an event will seem to echo something I talked about privately, and then I’ll notice someone from those circles sitting nearby or watching who I’m talking to. It starts to feel coordinated, like they’re quietly tracking where I am and who I’m meeting with.\n\nAnd even when I try to tell myself, “This could just be coincidence,” it doesn’t really settle it. I can’t let it go. It keeps feeling like the signals are meant for me, or at least connected to me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these signals or events are happening because of something you've done, or are they unrelated to your actions?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s connected to what I’m doing, mostly. Not like I did one single thing and then all of this started, but it seems tied to who I meet with and where I show up. Like if I have a client meeting, especially someone who overlaps with those finance or arts circles here in Cincinnati, then later an invitation comes through with wording that feels a little too close to what was discussed, or a panel topic will line up in a way that feels hard to brush off.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s unrelated. It feels like they’re reacting to my actions—tracking which clients I’m meeting with, who I’m talking to at events, where I’m sitting, that kind of thing. But when it keeps happening around my meetings and events, it’s hard for me not to feel like it’s because of what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you completely doubt these beliefs about being tracked, or do you generally feel convinced of them?\n\nPatient: I do have moments where I doubt it, yes. I mean, I’m not sitting here saying I have absolute proof or that every single invitation or every person sitting near me is definitely part of something. There are times when I’ll step back and think, “Okay, maybe this is just coincidence. It doesn’t leave my mind. Even when I tell myself it could just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people or events you think are tracking you are trying to communicate a specific message to you, or is it more about them monitoring your actions?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about monitoring me than, like, one clear message they’re trying to send. I don’t feel like there’s a single sentence I’m supposed to decode, or some direct instruction. It’s not like that.\n\nIt’s more that the invitations, the panel topics, even where people sit near me at events… it feels like these little signals that they know where I’ve been, who I’ve met with, what conversations I’ve had. Especially with certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati. They’re tracking this.”\n\nSo if there is a message, I guess it’s kind of implied: “We see what you’re doing.” But mostly it feels like being watched or followed quietly through these professional and social circles. And I know, again, I can tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, maybe these are broad topics and I’m reading into them, but I can’t really let it go once I notice the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like all these events and signals are connected by a single explanation or purpose?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do think there’s one kind of explanation behind it, or at least it feels that way to me. Not like a big dramatic purpose, exactly, but more like there’s a connected pattern: certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati seem to be keeping track of who I’m meeting with professionally, which clients I’m seeing, where I’m showing up, and then these small things happen afterward.\n\nLike, it isn’t just one invitation or one panel topic by itself. If it were only once, I could probably write it off. But when an invitation comes in with wording that reminds me of a client conversation, and then a panel topic lines up with something I was just dealing with, and then I notice someone sitting near me at an event who’s connected to those same circles… it starts to feel like it all points back to the same thing. Like the purpose is to let me know, quietly, that they’re aware of what I’m doing.\n\nI try to keep some part of myself saying, “Maybe this is coincidence, maybe these worlds are just small, maybe you’re reading too much into it.” But honestly, I came in because I can’t really let it go anymore. It feels connected enough that I keep turning it over in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people tracking you have abilities or technology beyond what normal people would have?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s like special technology or anything beyond normal people, at least not in the sci-fi sense. I’m not thinking they have some device or, you know, abilities that other people don’t have.\n\nIt feels more ordinary than that, which almost makes it harder for me to dismiss. Like, these are people in connected professional circles—arts, finance, events, client networks here in Cincinnati—and I keep thinking they could be keeping tabs through normal channels. Who talks to who, who gets invited where, what panels are being organized, who shows up and sits nearby. That kind of thing.\n\nSo no, not superhuman or advanced technology. More like… coordinated awareness, I guess. Certain people quietly tracking which clients I meet with and then little signals show up afterward through invitations or panel topics or who happens to be around me at events. And I do tell myself, “Maybe this is just coincidence, maybe those circles overlap more than I realize,” but once I notice it, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do your beliefs about being tracked change in intensity or certainty from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does change some. I mean, the basic belief is kind of always there in the background lately, like I haven’t really been able to just put it down and say, “That’s nothing.” That’s part of why I came in, because for a while now I’ve been convinced certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and then these little signals show up through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events.\n\nBut day to day, the intensity definitely goes up and down. If nothing happens for a day or two, or I’m busy with normal work stuff, I can sometimes tell myself, “Okay, maybe you’re overconnecting things, maybe it’s coincidence.” I can function and not think about it every minute.\n\nThen if I get an invitation that uses wording that reminds me of a client conversation, or I see a panel topic that lines up too closely with something I was just dealing with, it spikes again. Then I feel more certain, like, “No, this is connected, they know.” And even if part of me is saying it could just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go once I’ve noticed it. It’ll sit with me for the rest of the day, sometimes longer.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs affecting your ability to go about your daily routine or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is, though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely nonfunctional. I’m still working. I still have clients, I still show up for meetings, I still do the basic parts of being a financial advisor. But I’m not operating at the level I used to, if I’m being honest.\n\nMy practice has gotten smaller, and part of that is because I’ve been missing follow-ups or delaying them longer than I should. Like I’ll tell myself I’m going to call someone back or send the next email, and then I start thinking about whether that client is connected to someone, or whether something from that meeting is going to show up somewhere else, and I end up putting it off. Then a day becomes several days, and by then it’s awkward or I feel behind.\n\nI’ve also been avoiding some public panels and professional events I probably should be attending. Those used to be normal networking opportunities for me, and now I second-guess them. I’ll think, “Who’s going to be there? Why was I invited? Is this topic tied to something I said privately?” So I either don’t go, or I go and I’m distracted instead of actually making contacts.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting work. Not in a dramatic way where I can’t get out of bed or anything like that, but it’s definitely made me less consistent. I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage, and I think that’s started to show.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people tracking you are trying to harm you or is it more about them just watching you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly that they’re trying to physically harm me, if that’s what you mean. I’m not picturing someone jumping me or anything like that. It feels more like they’re watching, tracking, keeping tabs… but I guess the problem is that being watched like that doesn’t feel harmless.\n\nLike I’ve said, it’s these certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati, and I’ve gotten convinced they’re quietly paying attention to which clients I meet with. Then afterward I’ll notice these little signals—an invitation that seems worded a certain way, a panel topic that lines up too closely, or someone sitting near me at an event who feels connected to it. And even when I try to tell myself, “Okay, maybe this is coincidence,” I can’t really drop it.\n\nSo… harm, maybe not directly. But it feels like pressure. Like they’re letting me know they know. Like they’re watching how I move professionally. And that makes me guarded, because if they know who I’m meeting with, then I start wondering what else they know or what they’re going to do with it. But I wouldn’t say I have some clear idea that they’re planning to hurt me. It’s more the monitoring, the signaling, the being observed.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs about being tracked affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s affected things some, but honestly the clearest place I see it is work. I mean, outside of work I’m probably more distracted or guarded than I used to be, but when I try to point to actual consequences, it’s really my practice that’s taken the hit.\n\nI’m still working, still meeting clients, still doing the job, but it’s gotten smaller. I miss follow-ups, or I put them off because I start wondering whether that client is somehow connected to all this, or whether something from the meeting is going to show up later in some invitation or panel topic. And then I avoid some of the public panels and events too, which used to be part of how I stayed visible and kept relationships going professionally.\n\nSo with personal relationships… I don’t know, I wouldn’t say that’s been the main damage, at least not compared with work. The bigger issue is I’m not keeping up the same level of contact with clients that I used to, and that makes me feel like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there have been some changes, though it’s not like I’ve had some big falling-out with my family or anything. It’s more subtle than that. I still answer people, I still show up sometimes, but I’m just… less engaged, I guess.\n\nLike with friends, I used to go to jazz nights pretty regularly, or play pickup basketball if somebody texted the group. Lately I’ll just skip it, and the strange part is I don’t always feel that upset about missing it. I’ll see the message and think, “I should go,” and then it feels like too much effort to get myself there, or I start thinking about who might be around, or I just don’t have the same spark for it. So I end up staying home or doing something basic.\n\nAnd when friends ask what’s going on, I give these short answers. Not because I’m trying to be rude, exactly. It’s just that explaining everything feels exhausting, and even normal conversation feels like more effort than it should. I’ll say, “Yeah, I’m good, just busy,” or “Work’s been weird,” and then I leave it there.\n\nWith family, it’s similar. I’m not cutting them off, but I’m not as talkative. I’ll keep calls shorter than I used to. I still get things done in pieces, especially for work, but socially I feel like I’m kind of coasting. So yes, I think they’ve probably noticed I’m more distant, even if I haven’t really explained why.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy your free time or hobbies because of these concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m sitting there miserable the whole time, but there’s definitely less enjoyment than there used to be.\n\nI still get my work done, at least in pieces, but even there I can tell the energy isn’t the same. I’ll leave client follow-ups sitting longer than I should, not even because I made some clear decision to avoid them, but because I just don’t have that push I used to have. And then with free time it’s similar, maybe even more obvious, because those things are optional. If someone texts about a jazz night or pickup basketball, I used to feel interested right away. Now I’ll look at it and think, “Yeah, I should probably go,” but there’s no spark behind it. Sometimes I skip it and barely feel anything about skipping it, which is strange for me.\n\nPart of it is the concern about who might be there or whether something will connect back to work somehow, but part of it is just… talking, showing up, acting normal, all of that feels like more effort than it should. If friends ask what’s going on, I give these short answers, like “I’m fine” or “just busy,” because getting into it feels exhausting. So I end up staying home more, and even when I have free time, it doesn’t feel as refreshing or enjoyable as it used to.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or significantly distressed by these beliefs about being tracked on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yeah… overwhelmed might be a strong word every single minute of the day, but it is there most days. Like they’re letting me know they know.\n\nI can tell myself, “Okay, maybe that’s just coincidence, maybe you’re connecting dots that aren’t connected,” but the problem is I don’t really believe that enough to let it go. And I know, again, I can tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, maybe these are broad topics and I’m reading into them, but I can’t really let it go once I notice the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like all these events and signals are connected by a single explanation or purpose?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do think there’s one kind of explanation behind it, or at least it feels that way to me. Not like a big dramatic purpose, exactly, but more like there’s a connected pattern: certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati seem to be keeping track of who I’m meeting with professionally, which clients I’m seeing, where I’m showing up, and then these small things happen afterward.\n\nLike, it isn’t just one invitation or one panel topic by itself. If it were only once, I could probably write it off. But when an invitation comes in with wording that reminds me of a client conversation, and then a panel topic lines up with something I was just dealing with, and then I notice someone sitting near me at an event who’s connected to those same circles… it starts to feel like it all points back to the same thing. Like the purpose is to let me know, quietly, that they’re aware of what I’m doing.\n\nI try to keep some part of myself saying, “Maybe this is coincidence, maybe these worlds are just small, maybe you’re reading too much into it.” But honestly, I came in because I can’t really let it go anymore. It feels connected enough that I keep turning it over in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people tracking you have abilities or technology beyond what normal people would have?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s like special technology or anything beyond normal people, at least not in the sci-fi sense. I’m not thinking they have some device or, you know, abilities that other people don’t have.\n\nIt feels more ordinary than that, which almost makes it harder for me to dismiss. Like, these are people in connected professional circles—arts, finance, events, client networks here in Cincinnati—and I keep thinking they could be keeping tabs through normal channels. Who talks to who, who gets invited where, what panels are being organized, who shows up and sits nearby. That kind of thing.\n\nSo no, not superhuman or advanced technology. More like… coordinated awareness, I guess. Certain people quietly tracking which clients I meet with and then little signals show up afterward through invitations or panel topics or who happens to be around me at events. And I do tell myself, “Maybe this is just coincidence, maybe those circles overlap more than I realize,” but once I notice it, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do your beliefs about being tracked change in intensity or certainty from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does change some. I mean, the basic belief is kind of always there in the background lately, like I haven’t really been able to just put it down and say, “That’s nothing.” That’s part of why I came in, because for a while now I’ve been convinced certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and then these little signals show up through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events.\n\nBut day to day, the intensity definitely goes up and down. If nothing happens for a day or two, or I’m busy with normal work stuff, I can sometimes tell myself, “Okay, maybe you’re overconnecting things, maybe it’s coincidence.” I can function and not think about it every minute.\n\nThen if I get an invitation that uses wording that reminds me of a client conversation, or I see a panel topic that lines up too closely with something I was just dealing with, it spikes again. Then I feel more certain, like, “No, this is connected, they know.” And even if part of me is saying it could just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go once I’ve noticed it. It’ll sit with me for the rest of the day, sometimes longer.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs affecting your ability to go about your daily routine or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is, though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely nonfunctional. I’m still working. I still have clients, I still show up for meetings, I still do the basic parts of being a financial advisor. But I’m not operating at the level I used to, if I’m being honest.\n\nMy practice has gotten smaller, and part of that is because I’ve been missing follow-ups or delaying them longer than I should. Like I’ll tell myself I’m going to call someone back or send the next email, and then I start thinking about whether that client is connected to someone, or whether something from that meeting is going to show up somewhere else, and I end up putting it off. Then a day becomes several days, and by then it’s awkward or I feel behind.\n\nI’ve also been avoiding some public panels and professional events I probably should be attending. Those used to be normal networking opportunities for me, and now I second-guess them. I’ll think, “Who’s going to be there? Why was I invited? Is this topic tied to something I said privately?” So I either don’t go, or I go and I’m distracted instead of actually making contacts.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting work. Not in a dramatic way where I can’t get out of bed or anything like that, but it’s definitely made me less consistent. I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage, and I think that’s started to show.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people tracking you are trying to harm you or is it more about them just watching you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly that they’re trying to physically harm me, if that’s what you mean. I’m not picturing someone jumping me or anything like that. It feels more like they’re watching, tracking, keeping tabs… but I guess the problem is that being watched like that doesn’t feel harmless.\n\nLike I’ve said, it’s these certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati, and I’ve gotten convinced they’re quietly paying attention to which clients I meet with. Then afterward I’ll notice these little signals—an invitation that seems worded a certain way, a panel topic that lines up too closely, or someone sitting near me at an event who feels connected to it. And even when I try to tell myself, “Okay, maybe this is coincidence,” I can’t really drop it.\n\nSo… harm, maybe not directly. But it feels like pressure. Like they’re letting me know they know. Like they’re watching how I move professionally. And that makes me guarded, because if they know who I’m meeting with, then I start wondering what else they know or what they’re going to do with it. But I wouldn’t say I have some clear idea that they’re planning to hurt me. It’s more the monitoring, the signaling, the being observed.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs about being tracked affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s affected things some, but honestly the clearest place I see it is work. I mean, outside of work I’m probably more distracted or guarded than I used to be, but when I try to point to actual consequences, it’s really my practice that’s taken the hit.\n\nI’m still working, still meeting clients, still doing the job, but it’s gotten smaller. I miss follow-ups, or I put them off because I start wondering whether that client is somehow connected to all this, or whether something from the meeting is going to show up later in some invitation or panel topic. And then I avoid some of the public panels and events too, which used to be part of how I stayed visible and kept relationships going professionally.\n\nSo with personal relationships… I don’t know, I wouldn’t say that’s been the main damage, at least not compared with work. The bigger issue is I’m not keeping up the same level of contact with clients that I used to, and that makes me feel like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there have been some changes, though it’s not like I’ve had some big falling-out with my family or anything. It’s more subtle than that. I still answer people, I still show up sometimes, but I’m just… less engaged, I guess.\n\nLike with friends, I used to go to jazz nights pretty regularly, or play pickup basketball if somebody texted the group. Lately I’ll just skip it, and the strange part is I don’t always feel that upset about missing it. I’ll see the message and think, “I should go,” and then it feels like too much effort to get myself there, or I start thinking about who might be around, or I just don’t have the same spark for it. So I end up staying home or doing something basic.\n\nAnd when friends ask what’s going on, I give these short answers. Not because I’m trying to be rude, exactly. It’s just that explaining everything feels exhausting, and even normal conversation feels like more effort than it should. I’ll say, “Yeah, I’m good, just busy,” or “Work’s been weird,” and then I leave it there.\n\nWith family, it’s similar. I’m not cutting them off, but I’m not as talkative. I’ll keep calls shorter than I used to. I still get things done in pieces, especially for work, but socially I feel like I’m kind of coasting. So yes, I think they’ve probably noticed I’m more distant, even if I haven’t really explained why.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy your free time or hobbies because of these concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m sitting there miserable the whole time, but there’s definitely less enjoyment than there used to be.\n\nI still get my work done, at least in pieces, but even there I can tell the energy isn’t the same. I’ll leave client follow-ups sitting longer than I should, not even because I made some clear decision to avoid them, but because I just don’t have that push I used to have. And then with free time it’s similar, maybe even more obvious, because those things are optional. If someone texts about a jazz night or pickup basketball, I used to feel interested right away. Now I’ll look at it and think, “Yeah, I should probably go,” but there’s no spark behind it. Sometimes I skip it and barely feel anything about skipping it, which is strange for me.\n\nPart of it is the concern about who might be there or whether something will connect back to work somehow, but part of it is just… talking, showing up, acting normal, all of that feels like more effort than it should. If friends ask what’s going on, I give these short answers, like “I’m fine” or “just busy,” because getting into it feels exhausting. So I end up staying home more, and even when I have free time, it doesn’t feel as refreshing or enjoyable as it used to.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or significantly distressed by these beliefs about being tracked on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yeah… overwhelmed might be a strong word every single minute of the day, but it is there most days. It’s like this background pressure I keep carrying around.\n\nI came in because, for a while now, I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly keeping track of which clients I’m meeting with. And then I start noticing these little things — invitations that seem oddly timed, panel topics that sound a little too close to conversations I’ve had, or even who ends up sitting near me at events — and it feels like those things are signals. Like they’re letting me know they know.\n\nI can tell myself, “Okay, maybe that’s just coincidence, maybe you’re connecting dots that aren’t connected,” but the problem is I don’t really believe that enough to let it go. It keeps coming back. I’ll try to move on with my day, answer emails, see a client, do normal things, but part of my mind is still checking: does this fit, is this related, did someone know about that meeting?\n\nSo yes, it’s distressing. Maybe not in a dramatic way where I can’t function at all, but daily it wears on me. It makes me tense and guarded, and I spend more time thinking about it than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships or perform daily tasks because of your concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound like everything collapsed, because it hasn’t. I’m still working, I still have clients, I still get up and handle the basic things I need to handle. But it’s definitely harder than it used to be, and I can see the effect on my practice.\n\nMy client contact isn’t what it was. I’ll mean to follow up with someone after a meeting, or send a note, or schedule the next conversation, and then I put it off. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy, but if I’m being honest, part of it is that I start wondering who knows I met with them or whether that interaction is being noticed somehow. So a follow-up that should take ten minutes turns into something I avoid for a day, then two days, and then it feels awkward and I have to dig myself out of that.\n\nAnd I’ve been avoiding some of the more public-facing things too. Like panels or events where I’d normally go because it’s good for networking and just part of being visible in that world. Now I’ll think through who’s going to be there, who might be watching who I talk to, whether something I say could get repeated or connected back to a client. So I skip more of those than I used to.\n\nSo yes, my practice has gotten smaller in a way. Not overnight, not like I shut the door and stopped working, but smaller because I’m not keeping up the same level of contact. I’m not as consistent. Relationships with clients and professional contacts take regular attention, and I know I haven’t been giving that the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I don’t think of it as, “I can’t sleep because of that,” in a clean, obvious way.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came in because there’s this thing I haven’t been able to shake for a while now. I work in finance, and I’m around certain circles here in Cincinnati—clients, arts events, panels, fundraisers, that kind of thing—and I’ve gotten really convinced that some people in those arts and finance circles are sort of… keeping track of me. Like tracking which clients I meet with, who I talk to, where I show up.\n\nAnd it’s not always direct. It’s more like little signals. An invitation will come through and the wording feels pointed, or a panel topic will be weirdly close to something I just discussed with a client, or someone will sit near me at an event in a way that feels arranged. I know how that sounds, and I do tell myself, okay, maybe it’s coincidence, maybe I’m reading into it. But even when I try to talk myself down, I can’t really let it go. It keeps sticking in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these signals or invitations are specifically meant for you, or about you, rather than just coincidences?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, that’s the part I keep going back and forth on. On paper, I can say, sure, maybe invitations are just invitations, and panels have broad topics, and people sit wherever they sit. I understand that. I’m not completely unaware of how it sounds.\n\nBut in the moment, it feels like it’s specifically about me. Like the wording of an invitation will line up too closely with a client meeting I just had, or a topic at an event will seem to echo something I talked about privately, and then I’ll notice someone from those circles sitting nearby or watching who I’m talking to. It starts to feel coordinated, like they’re quietly tracking where I am and who I’m meeting with.\n\nAnd even when I try to tell myself, “This could just be coincidence,” it doesn’t really settle it. I can’t let it go. It keeps feeling like the signals are meant for me, or at least connected to me somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these signals or events are happening because of something you've done, or are they unrelated to your actions?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s connected to what I’m doing, mostly. Not like I did one single thing and then all of this started, but it seems tied to who I meet with and where I show up. Like if I have a client meeting, especially someone who overlaps with those finance or arts circles here in Cincinnati, then later an invitation comes through with wording that feels a little too close to what was discussed, or a panel topic will line up in a way that feels hard to brush off.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s unrelated. It feels like they’re reacting to my actions—tracking which clients I’m meeting with, who I’m talking to at events, where I’m sitting, that kind of thing. And then the “signals,” if that’s the right word, come through afterward. I know part of me can say, okay, maybe it’s coincidence, maybe I’m connecting dots that aren’t meant to be connected. But when it keeps happening around my meetings and events, it’s hard for me not to feel like it’s because of what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you completely doubt these beliefs about being tracked, or do you generally feel convinced of them?\n\nPatient: I do have moments where I doubt it, yes. I mean, I’m not sitting here saying I have absolute proof or that every single invitation or every person sitting near me is definitely part of something. There are times when I’ll step back and think, “Okay, maybe this is just coincidence. Maybe I’m overconnecting things because I’m stressed or because I’m around the same kinds of people a lot.”\n\nBut then something will happen again—like an invitation comes through with wording that feels too close to a client conversation, or a panel topic lines up with something I just dealt with at work, or I notice someone from those circles near me at an event—and it pulls me right back in. It feels like they’re quietly tracking which clients I meet with and where I’m showing up.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I can doubt it in theory, and I try to talk myself out of it, but I don’t really stop feeling convinced. It doesn’t leave my mind. Even when I tell myself it could just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people or events you think are tracking you are trying to communicate a specific message to you, or is it more about them monitoring your actions?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about monitoring me than, like, one clear message they’re trying to send. I don’t feel like there’s a single sentence I’m supposed to decode, or some direct instruction. It’s not like that.\n\nIt’s more that the invitations, the panel topics, even where people sit near me at events… it feels like these little signals that they know where I’ve been, who I’ve met with, what conversations I’ve had. Especially with certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati. Like I’ll meet with a client, and then later something comes through that seems to echo it too closely, and I start thinking, “Okay, they’re aware of that meeting. They’re tracking this.”\n\nSo if there is a message, I guess it’s kind of implied: “We see what you’re doing.” But mostly it feels like being watched or followed quietly through these professional and social circles. And I know, again, I can tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, maybe these are broad topics and I’m reading into them, but I can’t really let it go once I notice the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like all these events and signals are connected by a single explanation or purpose?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do think there’s one kind of explanation behind it, or at least it feels that way to me. Not like a big dramatic purpose, exactly, but more like there’s a connected pattern: certain people in those arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati seem to be keeping track of who I’m meeting with professionally, which clients I’m seeing, where I’m showing up, and then these small things happen afterward.\n\nLike, it isn’t just one invitation or one panel topic by itself. If it were only once, I could probably write it off. But when an invitation comes in with wording that reminds me of a client conversation, and then a panel topic lines up with something I was just dealing with, and then I notice someone sitting near me at an event who’s connected to those same circles… it starts to feel like it all points back to the same thing. Like the purpose is to let me know, quietly, that they’re aware of what I’m doing.\n\nI try to keep some part of myself saying, “Maybe this is coincidence, maybe these worlds are just small, maybe you’re reading too much into it.” But honestly, I came in because I can’t really let it go anymore. It feels connected enough that I keep turning it over in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people tracking you have abilities or technology beyond what normal people would have?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s like special technology or anything beyond normal people, at least not in the sci-fi sense. I’m not thinking they have some device or, you know, abilities that other people don’t have.\n\nIt feels more ordinary than that, which almost makes it harder for me to dismiss. Like, these are people in connected professional circles—arts, finance, events, client networks here in Cincinnati—and I keep thinking they could be keeping tabs through normal channels. Who talks to who, who gets invited where, what panels are being organized, who shows up and sits nearby. That kind of thing.\n\nSo no, not superhuman or advanced technology. More like… coordinated awareness, I guess. Certain people quietly tracking which clients I meet with and then little signals show up afterward through invitations or panel topics or who happens to be around me at events. And I do tell myself, “Maybe this is just coincidence, maybe those circles overlap more than I realize,” but once I notice it, I can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do your beliefs about being tracked change in intensity or certainty from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does change some. I mean, the basic belief is kind of always there in the background lately, like I haven’t really been able to just put it down and say, “That’s nothing.” That’s part of why I came in, because for a while now I’ve been convinced certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and then these little signals show up through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events.\n\nBut day to day, the intensity definitely goes up and down. If nothing happens for a day or two, or I’m busy with normal work stuff, I can sometimes tell myself, “Okay, maybe you’re overconnecting things, maybe it’s coincidence.” I can function and not think about it every minute.\n\nThen if I get an invitation that uses wording that reminds me of a client conversation, or I see a panel topic that lines up too closely with something I was just dealing with, it spikes again. Then I feel more certain, like, “No, this is connected, they know.” And even if part of me is saying it could just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go once I’ve noticed it. It’ll sit with me for the rest of the day, sometimes longer.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs affecting your ability to go about your daily routine or work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is, though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely nonfunctional. I’m still working. I still have clients, I still show up for meetings, I still do the basic parts of being a financial advisor. But I’m not operating at the level I used to, if I’m being honest.\n\nMy practice has gotten smaller, and part of that is because I’ve been missing follow-ups or delaying them longer than I should. Like I’ll tell myself I’m going to call someone back or send the next email, and then I start thinking about whether that client is connected to someone, or whether something from that meeting is going to show up somewhere else, and I end up putting it off. Then a day becomes several days, and by then it’s awkward or I feel behind.\n\nI’ve also been avoiding some public panels and professional events I probably should be attending. Those used to be normal networking opportunities for me, and now I second-guess them. I’ll think, “Who’s going to be there? Why was I invited? Is this topic tied to something I said privately?” So I either don’t go, or I go and I’m distracted instead of actually making contacts.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting work. Not in a dramatic way where I can’t get out of bed or anything like that, but it’s definitely made me less consistent. I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage, and I think that’s started to show.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people tracking you are trying to harm you or is it more about them just watching you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly that they’re trying to physically harm me, if that’s what you mean. I’m not picturing someone jumping me or anything like that. It feels more like they’re watching, tracking, keeping tabs… but I guess the problem is that being watched like that doesn’t feel harmless.\n\nLike I’ve said, it’s these certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati, and I’ve gotten convinced they’re quietly paying attention to which clients I meet with. Then afterward I’ll notice these little signals—an invitation that seems worded a certain way, a panel topic that lines up too closely, or someone sitting near me at an event who feels connected to it. And even when I try to tell myself, “Okay, maybe this is coincidence,” I can’t really drop it.\n\nSo… harm, maybe not directly. But it feels like pressure. Like they’re letting me know they know. Like they’re watching how I move professionally. And that makes me guarded, because if they know who I’m meeting with, then I start wondering what else they know or what they’re going to do with it. But I wouldn’t say I have some clear idea that they’re planning to hurt me. It’s more the monitoring, the signaling, the being observed.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs about being tracked affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s affected things some, but honestly the clearest place I see it is work. I mean, outside of work I’m probably more distracted or guarded than I used to be, but when I try to point to actual consequences, it’s really my practice that’s taken the hit.\n\nI’m still working, still meeting clients, still doing the job, but it’s gotten smaller. I miss follow-ups, or I put them off because I start wondering whether that client is somehow connected to all this, or whether something from the meeting is going to show up later in some invitation or panel topic. And then I avoid some of the public panels and events too, which used to be part of how I stayed visible and kept relationships going professionally.\n\nSo with personal relationships… I don’t know, I wouldn’t say that’s been the main damage, at least not compared with work. The bigger issue is I’m not keeping up the same level of contact with clients that I used to, and that makes me feel like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there have been some changes, though it’s not like I’ve had some big falling-out with my family or anything. It’s more subtle than that. I still answer people, I still show up sometimes, but I’m just… less engaged, I guess.\n\nLike with friends, I used to go to jazz nights pretty regularly, or play pickup basketball if somebody texted the group. Lately I’ll just skip it, and the strange part is I don’t always feel that upset about missing it. I’ll see the message and think, “I should go,” and then it feels like too much effort to get myself there, or I start thinking about who might be around, or I just don’t have the same spark for it. So I end up staying home or doing something basic.\n\nAnd when friends ask what’s going on, I give these short answers. Not because I’m trying to be rude, exactly. It’s just that explaining everything feels exhausting, and even normal conversation feels like more effort than it should. I’ll say, “Yeah, I’m good, just busy,” or “Work’s been weird,” and then I leave it there.\n\nWith family, it’s similar. I’m not cutting them off, but I’m not as talkative. I’ll keep calls shorter than I used to. I still get things done in pieces, especially for work, but socially I feel like I’m kind of coasting. So yes, I think they’ve probably noticed I’m more distant, even if I haven’t really explained why.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy your free time or hobbies because of these concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m sitting there miserable the whole time, but there’s definitely less enjoyment than there used to be.\n\nI still get my work done, at least in pieces, but even there I can tell the energy isn’t the same. I’ll leave client follow-ups sitting longer than I should, not even because I made some clear decision to avoid them, but because I just don’t have that push I used to have. And then with free time it’s similar, maybe even more obvious, because those things are optional. If someone texts about a jazz night or pickup basketball, I used to feel interested right away. Now I’ll look at it and think, “Yeah, I should probably go,” but there’s no spark behind it. Sometimes I skip it and barely feel anything about skipping it, which is strange for me.\n\nPart of it is the concern about who might be there or whether something will connect back to work somehow, but part of it is just… talking, showing up, acting normal, all of that feels like more effort than it should. If friends ask what’s going on, I give these short answers, like “I’m fine” or “just busy,” because getting into it feels exhausting. So I end up staying home more, and even when I have free time, it doesn’t feel as refreshing or enjoyable as it used to.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or significantly distressed by these beliefs about being tracked on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yeah… overwhelmed might be a strong word every single minute of the day, but it is there most days. It’s like this background pressure I keep carrying around.\n\nI came in because, for a while now, I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly keeping track of which clients I’m meeting with. And then I start noticing these little things — invitations that seem oddly timed, panel topics that sound a little too close to conversations I’ve had, or even who ends up sitting near me at events — and it feels like those things are signals. Like they’re letting me know they know.\n\nI can tell myself, “Okay, maybe that’s just coincidence, maybe you’re connecting dots that aren’t connected,” but the problem is I don’t really believe that enough to let it go. It keeps coming back. I’ll try to move on with my day, answer emails, see a client, do normal things, but part of my mind is still checking: does this fit, is this related, did someone know about that meeting?\n\nSo yes, it’s distressing. Maybe not in a dramatic way where I can’t function at all, but daily it wears on me. It makes me tense and guarded, and I spend more time thinking about it than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships or perform daily tasks because of your concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound like everything collapsed, because it hasn’t. I’m still working, I still have clients, I still get up and handle the basic things I need to handle. But it’s definitely harder than it used to be, and I can see the effect on my practice.\n\nMy client contact isn’t what it was. I’ll mean to follow up with someone after a meeting, or send a note, or schedule the next conversation, and then I put it off. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy, but if I’m being honest, part of it is that I start wondering who knows I met with them or whether that interaction is being noticed somehow. So a follow-up that should take ten minutes turns into something I avoid for a day, then two days, and then it feels awkward and I have to dig myself out of that.\n\nAnd I’ve been avoiding some of the more public-facing things too. Like panels or events where I’d normally go because it’s good for networking and just part of being visible in that world. Now I’ll think through who’s going to be there, who might be watching who I talk to, whether something I say could get repeated or connected back to a client. So I skip more of those than I used to.\n\nSo yes, my practice has gotten smaller in a way. Not overnight, not like I shut the door and stopped working, but smaller because I’m not keeping up the same level of contact. I’m not as consistent. Relationships with clients and professional contacts take regular attention, and I know I haven’t been giving that the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping because of these thoughts about being tracked?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I don’t think of it as, “I can’t sleep because of that,” in a clean, obvious way.\n\nSome nights I’ll lie there and my mind is still sort of turning things over from the day, and the tracking idea can be part of that, but I don’t know if that’s the main reason I’m awake or if it’s just normal stress and me overthinking. I haven’t really kept track of it closely. I wouldn’t want to say it’s a major sleep problem if I’m not sure.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes it keeps my mind busy at night, but I haven’t noticed a clear pattern where these thoughts are definitely ruining my sleep every night or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not keeping up the same level of client contact I used to manage, and I think that’s started to show.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the people tracking you are trying to harm you or is it more about them just watching you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly that they’re trying to physically harm me, if that’s what you mean. I’m not picturing someone jumping me or anything like that. It feels more like they’re watching, tracking, keeping tabs… but I guess the problem is that being watched like that doesn’t feel harmless.\n\nLike I’ve said, it’s these certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati, and I’ve gotten convinced they’re quietly paying attention to which clients I meet with. Then afterward I’ll notice these little signals—an invitation that seems worded a certain way, a panel topic that lines up too closely, or someone sitting near me at an event who feels connected to it. And even when I try to tell myself, “Okay, maybe this is coincidence,” I can’t really drop it.\n\nSo… harm, maybe not directly. But it feels like pressure. Like they’re letting me know they know. Like they’re watching how I move professionally. And that makes me guarded, because if they know who I’m meeting with, then I start wondering what else they know or what they’re going to do with it. But I wouldn’t say I have some clear idea that they’re planning to hurt me. It’s more the monitoring, the signaling, the being observed.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs about being tracked affecting your personal relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s affected things some, but honestly the clearest place I see it is work. I mean, outside of work I’m probably more distracted or guarded than I used to be, but when I try to point to actual consequences, it’s really my practice that’s taken the hit.\n\nI’m still working, still meeting clients, still doing the job, but it’s gotten smaller. I miss follow-ups, or I put them off because I start wondering whether that client is somehow connected to all this, or whether something from the meeting is going to show up later in some invitation or panel topic. And then I avoid some of the public panels and events too, which used to be part of how I stayed visible and kept relationships going professionally.\n\nSo with personal relationships… I don’t know, I wouldn’t say that’s been the main damage, at least not compared with work. The bigger issue is I’m not keeping up the same level of contact with clients that I used to, and that makes me feel like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any changes in your relationships with friends or family due to your concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there have been some changes, though it’s not like I’ve had some big falling-out with my family or anything. It’s more subtle than that. I still answer people, I still show up sometimes, but I’m just… less engaged, I guess.\n\nLike with friends, I used to go to jazz nights pretty regularly, or play pickup basketball if somebody texted the group. Lately I’ll just skip it, and the strange part is I don’t always feel that upset about missing it. I’ll see the message and think, “I should go,” and then it feels like too much effort to get myself there, or I start thinking about who might be around, or I just don’t have the same spark for it. So I end up staying home or doing something basic.\n\nAnd when friends ask what’s going on, I give these short answers. Not because I’m trying to be rude, exactly. It’s just that explaining everything feels exhausting, and even normal conversation feels like more effort than it should. I’ll say, “Yeah, I’m good, just busy,” or “Work’s been weird,” and then I leave it there.\n\nWith family, it’s similar. I’m not cutting them off, but I’m not as talkative. I’ll keep calls shorter than I used to. I still get things done in pieces, especially for work, but socially I feel like I’m kind of coasting. So yes, I think they’ve probably noticed I’m more distant, even if I haven’t really explained why.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy your free time or hobbies because of these concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m sitting there miserable the whole time, but there’s definitely less enjoyment than there used to be.\n\nI still get my work done, at least in pieces, but even there I can tell the energy isn’t the same. I’ll leave client follow-ups sitting longer than I should, not even because I made some clear decision to avoid them, but because I just don’t have that push I used to have. And then with free time it’s similar, maybe even more obvious, because those things are optional. If someone texts about a jazz night or pickup basketball, I used to feel interested right away. Now I’ll look at it and think, “Yeah, I should probably go,” but there’s no spark behind it. Sometimes I skip it and barely feel anything about skipping it, which is strange for me.\n\nPart of it is the concern about who might be there or whether something will connect back to work somehow, but part of it is just… talking, showing up, acting normal, all of that feels like more effort than it should. If friends ask what’s going on, I give these short answers, like “I’m fine” or “just busy,” because getting into it feels exhausting. So I end up staying home more, and even when I have free time, it doesn’t feel as refreshing or enjoyable as it used to.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or significantly distressed by these beliefs about being tracked on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yeah… overwhelmed might be a strong word every single minute of the day, but it is there most days. It’s like this background pressure I keep carrying around.\n\nI came in because, for a while now, I’ve been pretty convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly keeping track of which clients I’m meeting with. And then I start noticing these little things — invitations that seem oddly timed, panel topics that sound a little too close to conversations I’ve had, or even who ends up sitting near me at events — and it feels like those things are signals. Like they’re letting me know they know.\n\nI can tell myself, “Okay, maybe that’s just coincidence, maybe you’re connecting dots that aren’t connected,” but the problem is I don’t really believe that enough to let it go. It keeps coming back. I’ll try to move on with my day, answer emails, see a client, do normal things, but part of my mind is still checking: does this fit, is this related, did someone know about that meeting?\n\nSo yes, it’s distressing. Maybe not in a dramatic way where I can’t function at all, but daily it wears on me. It makes me tense and guarded, and I spend more time thinking about it than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships or perform daily tasks because of your concerns about being tracked?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound like everything collapsed, because it hasn’t. I’m still working, I still have clients, I still get up and handle the basic things I need to handle. But it’s definitely harder than it used to be, and I can see the effect on my practice.\n\nMy client contact isn’t what it was. I’ll mean to follow up with someone after a meeting, or send a note, or schedule the next conversation, and then I put it off. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just busy, but if I’m being honest, part of it is that I start wondering who knows I met with them or whether that interaction is being noticed somehow. So a follow-up that should take ten minutes turns into something I avoid for a day, then two days, and then it feels awkward and I have to dig myself out of that.\n\nAnd I’ve been avoiding some of the more public-facing things too. Like panels or events where I’d normally go because it’s good for networking and just part of being visible in that world. Now I’ll think through who’s going to be there, who might be watching who I talk to, whether something I say could get repeated or connected back to a client. So I skip more of those than I used to.\n\nSo yes, my practice has gotten smaller in a way. Not overnight, not like I shut the door and stopped working, but smaller because I’m not keeping up the same level of contact. I’m not as consistent. Relationships with clients and professional contacts take regular attention, and I know I haven’t been giving that the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping because of these thoughts about being tracked?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I don’t think of it as, “I can’t sleep because of that,” in a clean, obvious way.\n\nSome nights I’ll lie there and my mind is still sort of turning things over from the day, and the tracking idea can be part of that, but I don’t know if that’s the main reason I’m awake or if it’s just normal stress and me overthinking. I haven’t really kept track of it closely. I wouldn’t want to say it’s a major sleep problem if I’m not sure.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes it keeps my mind busy at night, but I haven’t noticed a clear pattern where these thoughts are definitely ruining my sleep every night or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it has made me more suspicious, maybe not of everyone in some blanket way, but more than I used to be. I don’t walk around thinking every single person is part of something, but when it comes to certain circles — especially the arts events, finance people, client-adjacent people here in Cincinnati — I catch myself wondering what they know or what they’re passing along.

Like if I get an invitation to something right after I’ve met with a client, or a panel topic seems oddly close to something I discussed privately, I don’t just see it as random anymore. I start thinking, okay, is that a signal? Are they trying to show me they know who I’ve been talking to? And then if someone sits near me at an event, or seems too interested in who I’m speaking with, I have a hard time trusting that it’s innocent.

I can tell myself, “Maybe you’re overreading this, maybe it’s coincidence,” and part of me knows that’s possible. But I don’t really settle there. I keep circling back to the idea that certain people are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. So yes, it affects trust. I’m more guarded. I measure what I say more carefully, and I’m less relaxed around people than I used to be.
